### Question 051: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 02-01 (can include mental status change, fever, and heada)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and molecular targets of prion diseases, specifically Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD), and differentiating it from other causes of rapidly progressive dementia. (理解朊病毒疾病，特别是克雅氏-格林斯坦综合征（CJD）的病理生理学和分子靶标，并将其与其他导致快速进展性痴呆的原因区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 6-month history of rapidly progressive cognitive decline, ataxia, and myoclonus. His wife reports that he initially experienced subtle memory problems but has deteriorated significantly over the past few months, now exhibiting severe confusion, difficulty walking, and involuntary muscle jerks. He has no significant past medical history other than hypertension, well-controlled with lisinopril. Neurological examination reveals marked dementia (MMSE score of 12/30), severe cerebellar ataxia (inability to perform finger-to-nose testing), and generalized myoclonus. Deep tendon reflexes are brisk, and plantar responses are extensor bilaterally. MRI of the brain shows diffuse cortical and basal ganglia ribboning of signal abnormalities on T2-weighted and FLAIR sequences, along with restricted diffusion in the caudate nucleus and putamen. CSF analysis reveals normal cell count and glucose, elevated protein (65 mg/dL), and a positive 14-3-3 protein test. EEG shows periodic sharp wave complexes. Which of the following molecular events is most directly responsible for the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Accumulation of phosphorylated tau protein forming neurofibrillary tangles.
(B) Misfolding and aggregation of the prion protein (PrPSc) leading to neuronal loss.
(C) Deposition of amyloid-beta plaques in the cerebral cortex and hippocampus.
(D) Formation of Lewy bodies composed primarily of alpha-synuclein in neurons.
(E) Autoimmune attack against neuronal surface antigens, such as NMDA receptors.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male. Age is relevant as sporadic CJD typically affects older adults.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Rapidly progressive cognitive decline (dementia), ataxia, myoclonus over 6 months. This rapid progression is a hallmark of CJD.
- **Neurological Exam:** Marked dementia (MMSE 12/30), severe cerebellar ataxia, generalized myoclonus, brisk reflexes, extensor plantar responses (pyramidal signs). This constellation points towards a diffuse, rapidly progressive neurodegenerative process affecting multiple brain regions.
- **MRI Findings:** Diffuse cortical and basal ganglia ribboning on T2/FLAIR, restricted diffusion in caudate/putamen. These are characteristic MRI findings in sporadic CJD.
- **CSF Analysis:** Normal cell count/glucose, elevated protein, positive 14-3-3 protein. Elevated 14-3-3 protein is a sensitive marker for CJD, although not entirely specific.
- **EEG Findings:** Periodic sharp wave complexes. This is a highly specific, though not always present, finding in sporadic CJD.
- **Overall Picture:** The clinical presentation (rapid dementia, ataxia, myoclonus), MRI findings, CSF markers (14-3-3), and EEG findings strongly suggest Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *molecular event* responsible for the pathogenesis. This requires identifying the underlying cause of CJD.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **CJD Pathogenesis:** CJD is a prion disease caused by the misfolding of the normal cellular prion protein (PrPC) into an abnormal, infectious isoform called the scrapie prion protein (PrPSc).
- **PrPC vs. PrPSc:** PrPC is a normal glycoprotein found predominantly on the surface of neurons, with functions potentially involving cell signaling, copper metabolism, and synaptic plasticity. PrPSc has the same amino acid sequence but a different secondary and tertiary structure (richer in beta-sheets), making it resistant to proteases and prone to aggregation.
- **Mechanism:** PrPSc acts as a template, inducing the conformational change of normal PrPC molecules into more PrPSc. This leads to a chain reaction of misfolding and aggregation.
- **Consequences:** The accumulation of PrPSc aggregates causes neuronal dysfunction, synaptic loss, spongiform degeneration (vacuolation) of the neuropil, gliosis, and ultimately neuronal death. This process leads to the characteristic clinical and pathological features of CJD.
- **Molecular Target:** The primary molecular event is the misfolding and aggregation of the prion protein (PrPSc).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Accumulation of phosphorylated tau protein forming neurofibrillary tangles: This is the hallmark of Alzheimer's disease and other tauopathies. While Alzheimer's causes dementia, it typically progresses much more slowly than CJD, and the clinical features (ataxia, myoclonus) and MRI/EEG findings are not characteristic.
- (B) Misfolding and aggregation of the prion protein (PrPSc) leading to neuronal loss: This accurately describes the pathogenesis of CJD. The clinical presentation, MRI, CSF, and EEG findings are all consistent with CJD, which is caused by PrPSc.
- (C) Deposition of amyloid-beta plaques in the cerebral cortex and hippocampus: This is the hallmark of Alzheimer's disease. As with option A, the clinical course and specific findings (ataxia, myoclonus, MRI ribboning, EEG complexes) are inconsistent with Alzheimer's disease.
- (D) Formation of Lewy bodies composed primarily of alpha-synuclein in neurons: This is the hallmark of Parkinson's disease and Dementia with Lewy Bodies (DLB). While DLB can cause dementia, the rapid progression, prominent ataxia, myoclonus, and specific MRI/EEG findings are not typical. Parkinson's primarily involves motor symptoms.
- (E) Autoimmune attack against neuronal surface antigens, such as NMDA receptors: This describes autoimmune encephalitis, such as anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis. While this can cause rapidly progressive psychiatric symptoms, cognitive decline, and neurological deficits, the specific combination of ataxia, myoclonus, characteristic MRI findings (ribboning, diffusion restriction), positive 14-3-3, and periodic sharp waves on EEG is highly specific for CJD, not autoimmune encephalitis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | CJD (Prion Disease)                                  | Alzheimer's Disease (Tau/Amyloid)                 | Parkinson's/DLB (Alpha-Synuclein)                 | Autoimmune Encephalitis (Anti-NMDA Receptor) |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Pathology**   | PrPSc misfolding & aggregation                       | Amyloid plaques & Neurofibrillary tangles (Tau) | Lewy bodies (Alpha-Synuclein)                     | Autoantibodies against neuronal antigens        |
| **Progression**         | Rapid (weeks to months)                              | Slow (years)                                      | Variable (often slower)                           | Rapid (days to weeks)                          |
| **Key Symptoms**        | Dementia, Ataxia, Myoclonus                          | Memory loss, Cognitive decline                    | Parkinsonism, Fluctuating cognition, Hallucinations | Psychiatric symptoms, Seizures, Cognitive decline |
| **MRI**                 | Cortical/BG ribboning, Diffusion restriction (caudate) | Atrophy (hippocampal, cortical)                   | Variable, may show atrophy                        | Variable, may show inflammation                 |
| **CSF**                 | Positive 14-3-3, Elevated protein                    | Normal or slightly elevated protein, low Aβ42     | Normal                                            | Elevated protein, pleocytosis (sometimes)       |
| **EEG**                 | Periodic sharp wave complexes                        | Slowing                                           | Slowing                                           | Slowing, epileptiform activity                |
| **Molecular Target**    | Prion Protein (PrPC -> PrPSc)                        | Tau protein, Amyloid-beta protein                 | Alpha-Synuclein                                   | Neuronal surface antigens (e.g., NMDA receptor) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD) is a rapidly progressive neurodegenerative disorder caused by the misfolding of the prion protein (PrPC) into its pathogenic isoform (PrPSc). Key diagnostic features include rapid dementia, ataxia, myoclonus, characteristic MRI findings (cortical ribboning, basal ganglia diffusion restriction), positive CSF 14-3-3 protein, and periodic sharp wave complexes on EEG. Differentiating CJD from other causes of rapidly progressive dementia (like Alzheimer's, autoimmune encephalitis) requires careful consideration of the clinical presentation, neuroimaging, CSF markers, and EEG findings. The core molecular pathology involves the conformational change and aggregation of PrPSc.

### Question 052: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 02-02 (• Arthropod borne forms can be due to St. Louis, E)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis and characteristic neuropathological findings of St. Louis Encephalitis (SLE), an arthropod-borne flavivirus infection affecting the central nervous system. (理解圣路易斯脑炎的病理发生机制和特征性神经病理学发现，一种影响中枢神经系统的媒介传播黄病毒感染。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male farmer living in rural Missouri presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, severe headache, nausea, and vomiting. He reports being bitten by mosquitoes frequently over the past week. His past medical history is significant for hypertension, managed with lisinopril, and type 2 diabetes mellitus, managed with metformin. On examination, he is febrile (39.2°C), tachycardic (110 bpm), and appears lethargic with mild nuchal rigidity. Neurological examination reveals disorientation, generalized muscle weakness, and absent deep tendon reflexes in the lower extremities. A lumbar puncture shows cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) with elevated protein (150 mg/dL, normal <45 mg/dL), normal glucose (60 mg/dL, normal 40-100 mg/dL), and pleocytosis with 250 cells/µL, predominantly lymphocytes (80%). CSF PCR for enteroviruses is negative. Brain MRI shows T2/FLAIR hyperintensities in the basal ganglia, thalamus, and brainstem. CSF IgM antibody testing for St. Louis encephalitis virus is positive.

Which of the following pathological findings is MOST characteristic of the brain tissue in this patient?

(A) Perivascular lymphocytic cuffing and microglial nodules surrounding neurons, with neuronal loss and gliosis, predominantly in the cerebral cortex.
(B) Intranuclear eosinophilic inclusions (Cowdry type A) within neurons and glial cells, primarily affecting the temporal and insular lobes.
(C) Perivascular lymphocytic cuffing and microglial nodules surrounding neurons, with neuronal loss and gliosis, predominantly in the basal ganglia, thalamus, and brainstem.
(D) Formation of syncytial giant cells with intranuclear inclusions in the cytoplasm, leading to demyelination and axonal damage, primarily in the white matter.
(E) Diffuse neuronal loss and gliosis with spongiform changes and amyloid plaque deposition, predominantly in the cerebral cortex and hippocampus.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male farmer in Missouri (endemic area for SLE).
*   **Symptoms:** Acute onset fever, severe headache, nausea, vomiting, lethargy, nuchal rigidity, disorientation, muscle weakness, absent reflexes. This constellation points towards encephalitis.
*   **Exposure:** Mosquito bites (vector for SLE).
*   **CSF Findings:** Elevated protein, normal glucose, lymphocytic pleocytosis. This is typical for viral encephalitis. Negative enterovirus PCR helps rule out a common cause.
*   **Neuroimaging:** MRI showing T2/FLAIR hyperintensities in the basal ganglia, thalamus, and brainstem. This pattern is highly suggestive of SLE.
*   **Serology:** Positive CSF IgM for St. Louis encephalitis virus confirms the diagnosis.
*   **Question:** Asks for the characteristic pathological findings in the brain tissue.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   St. Louis encephalitis (SLE) is caused by the St. Louis encephalitis virus (SLEV), a flavivirus transmitted by *Culex* mosquitoes.
*   The virus primarily targets neurons and glial cells in the central nervous system.
*   Pathologically, SLE causes a perivascular inflammatory response characterized by lymphocytic cuffing around small blood vessels.
*   Microglial nodules (aggregates of activated microglia) form around damaged neurons.
*   Neuronal loss (necrosis and apoptosis) and reactive gliosis (astrocytosis) are prominent features.
*   Crucially, SLE preferentially affects specific brain regions, including the basal ganglia (especially the caudate nucleus and putamen), thalamus, brainstem, and cerebellum. The cerebral cortex is typically spared or minimally involved.
*   The clinical presentation (fever, headache, altered mental status, motor deficits, absent reflexes) correlates well with the involvement of these specific brain structures. Damage to the basal ganglia and thalamus explains the movement disorders and altered consciousness, while brainstem involvement can lead to cranial nerve palsies and autonomic dysfunction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A)** Describes findings typical of Herpes Simplex Virus (HSV) encephalitis. HSV encephalitis characteristically involves the temporal and insular lobes of the cerebral cortex, leading to hemorrhagic necrosis, perivascular lymphocytic cuffing, microglial nodules, and neuronal loss. The location (cerebral cortex) is incorrect for SLE.
*   **(B)** Describes findings typical of Herpes Simplex Virus (HSV) encephalitis. Cowdry type A intranuclear inclusions are pathognomonic for HSV infection. The location (temporal/insular lobes) is incorrect for SLE.
*   **(D)** Describes findings typical of Progressive Multifocal Leukoencephalopathy (PML), caused by the JC virus. PML primarily affects oligodendrocytes in the white matter, leading to demyelination, axonal damage, and the formation of syncytial giant cells with intranuclear inclusions. The location (white matter) and specific pathology (demyelination, giant cells) are incorrect for SLE.
*   **(E)** Describes findings typical of Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD), a prion disease. CJD is characterized by spongiform changes (vacuolation) in the neuropil, neuronal loss, gliosis, and amyloid plaque deposition, predominantly in the cerebral cortex and cerebellum. The specific pathology (spongiform changes, amyloid plaques) is incorrect for SLE.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | St. Louis Encephalitis (SLE) | HSV Encephalitis | PML                      | CJD                      |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------- | :--------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- |
| **Etiology**            | Flavivirus (SLEV)            | HSV-1            | JC Virus                | Prion (PrPSc)            |
| **Transmission**        | Mosquito (*Culex*)           | Reactivation/Primary | Immunocompromised state | Sporadic/Genetic/Iatrogenic |
| **Typical Location**    | Basal ganglia, Thalamus, Brainstem | Temporal/Insular Cortex | White Matter            | Cortex, Cerebellum       |
| **Key Pathology**       | Perivascular cuffing, Microglial nodules, Neuronal loss/gliosis | Hemorrhagic necrosis, Perivascular cuffing, Microglial nodules, Cowdry A inclusions | Demyelination, Giant cells with inclusions | Spongiform changes, Amyloid plaques |
| **CSF Findings**        | Lymphocytic pleocytosis, Elevated protein | Lymphocytic pleocytosis, Elevated protein, RBCs | Lymphocytic pleocytosis, Elevated protein | Normal or mild pleocytosis |
| **MRI Findings**        | T2/FLAIR hyperintensity in BG, Thalamus, Brainstem | T2/FLAIR hyperintensity in Temporal/Insular lobes, Hemorrhage | Asymmetric white matter T2/FLAIR hyperintensities | Cortical ribboning, Basal ganglia hyperintensity |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
St. Louis encephalitis is a flavivirus infection transmitted by mosquitoes, causing encephalitis with a characteristic pathological pattern involving perivascular lymphocytic inflammation, microglial nodules, neuronal loss, and gliosis, predominantly affecting the basal ganglia, thalamus, and brainstem. This pattern distinguishes it from other viral encephalitides like HSV (temporal lobe involvement, Cowdry A inclusions) and PML (white matter demyelination).

### Question 053: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 02-03 (equine, and Venezuelan encephalitides.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the distinct neuropathological mechanisms and resulting lesions caused by different alphaviruses, specifically equine encephalitis virus (EEEV) and Venezuelan equine encephalitis virus (VEEV), focusing on their tropism and effects on neuronal populations. (理解不同甲型病毒（如马脑炎病毒和委内瑞拉马脑炎病毒）的独特神经病理机制和由此产生的病变，重点关注它们的神经亲和性和对神经元群体的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male farmer in rural Texas presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, severe headache, nausea, and progressive confusion. He reports being bitten by mosquitoes approximately one week prior to symptom onset. His past medical history is significant for hypertension, managed with lisinopril. On examination, his temperature is 39.5°C (103.1°F), blood pressure is 140/90 mmHg, heart rate is 110 bpm, and respiratory rate is 20 breaths/min. Neurological examination reveals disorientation to time and place, generalized muscle weakness, and positive Babinski signs bilaterally. A lumbar puncture shows cloudy cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) with an elevated white blood cell count (500 cells/µL, predominantly lymphocytes), elevated protein (150 mg/dL), and normal glucose (60 mg/dL). CSF PCR testing is positive for an alphavirus. Brain MRI reveals T2-weighted hyperintensities predominantly affecting the thalamus, basal ganglia, and brainstem. The patient's condition rapidly deteriorates over the next 48 hours, leading to coma and death despite supportive care. Autopsy of the brain reveals widespread neuronal necrosis and microglial nodules primarily in the thalamus, basal ganglia, and brainstem. Immunohistochemical staining shows positive viral antigen within these affected neurons and glial cells.

Which of the following pathological mechanisms BEST explains the specific pattern of neuronal damage observed in this patient?

(A) Formation of syncytia via fusion of infected cells mediated by viral fusion proteins, leading to widespread demyelination.
(B) Direct viral cytopathic effect causing necrosis of neurons and glial cells, particularly targeting specific brain regions like the thalamus and basal ganglia.
(C) Immune-mediated demyelination triggered by molecular mimicry between viral antigens and myelin basic protein, resulting in multifocal white matter lesions.
(D) Apoptosis of neurons induced by viral proteins that activate caspase pathways, primarily affecting the cerebral cortex and hippocampus.
(E) Formation of neuronal inclusions containing viral proteins, leading to chronic inflammation and progressive neurodegeneration, characteristic of prion diseases.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old farmer in Texas, suggesting exposure to vector-borne diseases prevalent in the region.
*   **Symptoms:** Acute onset fever, headache, confusion, muscle weakness, positive Babinski signs – indicative of acute encephalitis.
*   **Exposure:** Mosquito bite one week prior – points towards an arboviral etiology.
*   **CSF Findings:** Cloudy CSF, lymphocytic pleocytosis, elevated protein, normal glucose – consistent with viral encephalitis.
*   **CSF PCR:** Positive for alphavirus – narrows down the potential causative agents (e.g., EEEV, VEEV, Western equine encephalitis virus (WEV), Eastern equine encephalitis virus (EEEV)).
*   **Brain MRI:** T2 hyperintensities in thalamus, basal ganglia, brainstem – characteristic imaging findings for certain types of viral encephalitis, particularly EEEV and VEEV.
*   **Clinical Course:** Rapid deterioration to coma and death – suggests a severe, fulminant form of encephalitis.
*   **Autopsy Findings:** Widespread neuronal necrosis and microglial nodules in thalamus, basal ganglia, brainstem – confirms severe neuronal damage in specific brain regions.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Positive viral antigen in affected neurons and glial cells – confirms direct viral infection and cytopathic effect.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the BEST pathological mechanism explaining the specific pattern of neuronal damage.

The combination of clinical presentation (acute encephalitis), exposure history (mosquito bite), CSF findings (viral encephalitis), MRI findings (thalamus, basal ganglia, brainstem involvement), and autopsy findings (neuronal necrosis in these regions) strongly points towards severe alphaviral encephalitis, likely caused by EEEV or VEEV, given the geographic location and specific brain regions affected. The key pathological mechanism for these viruses is direct viral cytopathic effect leading to neuronal necrosis.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Alphaviruses (EEEV, VEEV):** These are positive-sense, single-stranded RNA viruses transmitted by mosquitoes.
*   **Pathogenesis:** After entering the CNS, alphaviruses like EEEV and VEEV exhibit neurotropism, preferentially infecting neurons and glial cells in specific brain regions, notably the thalamus, basal ganglia, brainstem, and cerebellum.
*   **Mechanism of Damage:** The primary mechanism of neuronal damage is direct viral cytopathic effect. The virus replicates within neurons, leading to cell swelling, disruption of cellular processes, and ultimately, cell death (necrosis). This contrasts with some other viruses that primarily cause damage through immune responses (e.g., immune-mediated demyelination) or apoptosis.
*   **Histopathology:** Autopsy findings typically show perivascular lymphocytic infiltrates, microglial nodules, and widespread neuronal necrosis and gliosis, particularly concentrated in the thalamus, basal ganglia, brainstem, and cerebellum.
*   **Clinical Correlation:** The specific brain regions affected correlate with the clinical manifestations. Thalamic involvement contributes to altered consciousness and coma. Basal ganglia involvement can lead to movement disorders. Brainstem involvement affects vital functions.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Formation of syncytia via fusion of infected cells mediated by viral fusion proteins, leading to widespread demyelination.** While some viruses (like measles virus causing SSPE or HIV-1 encephalitis) cause syncytia formation, and demyelination can occur in viral encephalitis (e.g., JC virus causing PML), this is not the primary mechanism for EEEV/VEEV. EEEV/VEEV primarily cause neuronal necrosis, not widespread demyelination or syncytia formation as the main pathology. Demyelination is more characteristic of immune-mediated processes or specific viruses like JC virus.
- (C): **Immune-mediated demyelination triggered by molecular mimicry between viral antigens and myelin basic protein, resulting in multifocal white matter lesions.** This mechanism is characteristic of Guillain-Barré syndrome (following Campylobacter jejuni infection) or potentially some forms of post-infectious encephalomyelitis. While inflammation is present in alphaviral encephalitis, the primary damage is direct neuronal necrosis, not immune-mediated demyelination targeting white matter tracts. The MRI findings also point to grey matter structures (thalamus, basal ganglia) rather than primarily white matter.
- (D): **Apoptosis of neurons induced by viral proteins that activate caspase pathways, primarily affecting the cerebral cortex and hippocampus.** Apoptosis can be a mechanism of neuronal death in some viral infections, but the predominant mechanism in severe EEEV/VEEV encephalitis is necrosis. Furthermore, the primary targets are the thalamus, basal ganglia, and brainstem, not predominantly the cortex and hippocampus.
- (E): **Formation of neuronal inclusions containing viral proteins, leading to chronic inflammation and progressive neurodegeneration, characteristic of prion diseases.** Neuronal inclusions are characteristic of certain viral infections (e.g., rabies Negri bodies, CMV owl's eye inclusions) and neurodegenerative diseases (e.g., Lewy bodies in Parkinson's, tau tangles in Alzheimer's, prion protein aggregates in CJD). This is not the mechanism of damage caused by EEEV/VEEV, which cause acute, fulminant necrosis. Prion diseases are chronic and progressive.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | EEEV/VEEV Encephalitis (Correct) | Measles SSPE (Distractor A) | JC Virus PML (Distractor A, C) | GBS (Distractor C) | Prion Disease (Distractor E) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :----------------- | :--------------------------- |
| **Primary Pathology**   | Neuronal Necrosis                 | Syncytia, Demyelination     | Demyelination                  | Demyelination      | Neuronal Loss, Inclusions    |
| **Mechanism**           | Direct Cytopathic Effect          | Viral Fusion               | Immune-mediated                | Immune-mediated    | Protein Misfolding            |
| **Brain Regions Affected** | Thalamus, Basal Ganglia, Brainstem | White Matter               | White Matter                   | Peripheral Nerves | Cortex, Cerebellum           |
| **Clinical Course**     | Acute, Fulminant                  | Chronic, Progressive        | Subacute, Progressive          | Acute/Subacute    | Chronic, Progressive          |
| **Key Histology**       | Neuronal Necrosis, Microglial Nodules | Multinucleated Giant Cells | Oligodendrocyte Infection/Loss | Demyelination      | Spongiform Change, Plaques   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Alphaviruses like EEEV and VEEV cause severe encephalitis characterized by direct viral cytopathic effect leading to neuronal necrosis, particularly in the thalamus, basal ganglia, and brainstem. This contrasts with other viral CNS infections that may primarily cause demyelination (JC virus, immune-mediated), apoptosis (some viruses), or syncytia formation (measles). Understanding the specific tropism and pathogenic mechanism of different neurotropic viruses is crucial for diagnosis and understanding the resulting clinical and pathological patterns.

<a id='question-054'></a>

### Question 054: Central Nervous System Pathology: Herpes Simplex Encephalitis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Herpes simplex virus (HSV) encephalitis, particularly HSV-1, characteristically affects the temporal and frontal lobes, causing hemorrhagic necrosis and neuronal damage, often associated with specific histopathological findings like Cowdry type A inclusions and ballooning degeneration. (单纯疱疹病毒（HSV）脑炎，特别是HSV-1，特征性地影响颞叶和额叶，导致出血性坏死和神经元损伤，通常与Cowdry A型包涵体和气球样变性等特定组织病理学发现有关。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of worsening headache, fever (39.4°C), confusion, and focal seizures involving his right arm. He is currently taking lisinopril and atorvastatin. Neurological examination reveals altered mental status (Glasgow Coma Scale score of 11), right-sided hemiparesis, and expressive aphasia. A non-contrast head CT scan shows hypodensity in the left temporal and inferior frontal lobes. Lumbar puncture reveals cloudy cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) with elevated protein (150 mg/dL, normal <45 mg/dL), decreased glucose (30 mg/dL, normal 50-80 mg/dL relative to serum glucose), and pleocytosis with 500 cells/µL (predominantly lymphocytes). CSF PCR for herpes simplex virus (HSV) is positive. MRI of the brain confirms the CT findings and shows T2/FLAIR hyperintensity and restricted diffusion in the affected regions, with associated gyral enhancement after gadolinium administration. Brain biopsy is performed. Histopathological examination reveals neuronal necrosis, inflammation, and characteristic intranuclear eosinophilic inclusions within neurons and glial cells.

Which of the following histopathological findings is most characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Lewy bodies within surviving neurons
(B) Amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles
(C) Cowdry type A intranuclear inclusions
(D) Rosenthal fibers within astrocytes
(E) Negri bodies within pyramidal neurons

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male with hypertension and hyperlipidemia. This demographic is relevant but doesn't point directly to a specific pathology.
- **Symptoms:** Acute onset (3 days) of headache, fever, confusion, focal seizures (right arm), altered mental status (GCS 11), right hemiparesis, expressive aphasia. This constellation points towards an acute neurological process, likely encephalitis or stroke, affecting the left hemisphere (given right-sided deficits and aphasia).
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast CT shows hypodensity in left temporal and inferior frontal lobes. MRI confirms T2/FLAIR hyperintensity, restricted diffusion, and gyral enhancement in the same regions. These findings are highly suggestive of acute encephalitis with hemorrhagic necrosis, particularly involving the temporal and frontal lobes, which is characteristic of HSV encephalitis.
- **CSF Analysis:** Cloudy CSF, elevated protein, decreased glucose, pleocytosis (lymphocytes). This indicates inflammation within the CNS, consistent with meningitis or encephalitis. The decreased glucose suggests bacterial or viral (like HSV) etiology over typical bacterial meningitis (though lymphocytes are more typical for viral).
- **CSF PCR:** Positive for HSV. This confirms the diagnosis of HSV encephalitis.
- **Histopathology:** Neuronal necrosis, inflammation, and characteristic intranuclear eosinophilic inclusions. The question asks for the *most characteristic* histopathological finding.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **HSV Encephalitis Pathogenesis:** HSV, typically HSV-1 in immunocompetent adults, enters the CNS via olfactory or trigeminal nerves. It replicates in neurons, causing direct cytopathic effects (neuronal necrosis, inflammation) and an inflammatory response. The virus has a predilection for the temporal and inferior frontal lobes.
- **Histopathology:** The hallmark histopathological features include neuronal necrosis, perivascular lymphocytic infiltrate, microglial nodules, and characteristic intranuclear inclusions. These inclusions are eosinophilic, homogenous, and surrounded by a clear halo, known as Cowdry type A inclusions. Ballooning degeneration of neurons and astrocytes can also be seen.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** HSV DNA polymerase is the target for antiviral drugs like acyclovir. The virus replicates within the nucleus, leading to the formation of Cowdry type A inclusions, which represent aggregates of viral proteins and nucleic acids.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Lewy bodies** are eosinophilic cytoplasmic inclusions containing α-synuclein, characteristic of Parkinson disease and Lewy body dementia. They are found in surviving neurons, not typically associated with acute viral encephalitis.
- (B): **Amyloid plaques** (extracellular deposits of amyloid-beta peptide) and **neurofibrillary tangles** (intracellular aggregates of hyperphosphorylated tau protein) are the hallmark pathological features of Alzheimer's disease, a chronic neurodegenerative condition.
- (C): **Cowdry type A intranuclear inclusions** are eosinophilic, homogenous inclusions surrounded by a clear halo, found within the nucleus of infected cells. They are characteristic of HSV infections (encephalitis, keratitis, gingivostomatitis), varicella-zoster virus (VZV) infections, and sometimes adenovirus infections. Given the clinical presentation and confirmed HSV PCR, this is the most characteristic finding.
- (D): **Rosenthal fibers** are thick, elongated, eosinophilic structures found within the cytoplasm of astrocytes. They are characteristic of Alexander disease, a rare leukodystrophy, and can also be seen in pilocytic astrocytomas and aging brains, but not typically in HSV encephalitis.
- (E): **Negri bodies** are eosinophilic, sharply outlined, intracytoplasmic inclusions found in the cytoplasm of pyramidal neurons in the hippocampus and cerebral cortex. They are pathognomonic for rabies virus infection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | HSV Encephalitis (Correct Answer) | Alzheimer's Disease | Parkinson Disease | Rabies | Alexander Disease |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :------------------ | :---------------- | :----- | :---------------- |
| **Characteristic Lesion** | Cowdry type A intranuclear inclusions | Amyloid plaques, Neurofibrillary tangles | Lewy bodies | Negri bodies | Rosenthal fibers |
| **Cell Type**           | Neurons, Glial cells               | Neurons             | Neurons           | Neurons | Astrocytes        |
| **Location**            | Temporal/Frontal lobes             | Hippocampus, Cortex | Substantia nigra  | Hippocampus, Cortex | White matter      |
| **Etiology**            | Herpes Simplex Virus              | Amyloid-beta, Tau   | α-synuclein       | Rabies virus | Genetic (GFAP)    |
| **Clinical Context**    | Acute encephalitis                | Chronic dementia    | Movement disorder | Acute encephalitis | Progressive neurological decline |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- High-Yield Takeaway: HSV encephalitis characteristically affects the temporal and frontal lobes and shows Cowdry type A intranuclear inclusions on brain biopsy.
- Differentials: Remember the characteristic inclusions for other CNS pathologies: Lewy bodies (Parkinson's), Amyloid plaques/NFTs (Alzheimer's), Negri bodies (Rabies), Rosenthal fibers (Alexander disease). Knowing these helps differentiate between various neurological conditions based on histopathology.

<a id='question-055'></a>

### Question 055: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 02-05 (temporal lobes. Cowdry type A bodies are intranucle)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identification of the causative agent and associated neuropathological findings (Cowdry type A bodies) in a patient presenting with acute encephalitis, specifically Herpes Simplex Virus (HSV) encephalitis, and its characteristic localization to the temporal lobes. (识别导致性脑炎的病原体及其相关的病理发现（A型柯尔德里小体），特别是单纯疱疹病毒（HSV）脑炎，及其在颞叶的特征性定位。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of worsening headache, fever (39.4°C), confusion, and focal seizures involving his right arm. He is currently taking lisinopril and atorvastatin. On examination, he is lethargic, disoriented to time and place, and exhibits right-sided hemiparesis. Cranial nerve examination is grossly intact. A non-contrast head CT scan shows subtle hypodensity in the left medial temporal lobe. Lumbar puncture reveals cloudy cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) with elevated protein (150 mg/dL, normal <45 mg/dL), decreased glucose (30 mg/dL, normal 50-80 mg/dL), and pleocytosis with 500 cells/µL (predominantly lymphocytes). CSF PCR for Herpes Simplex Virus (HSV) type 1 is positive. Brain biopsy is performed, and histopathological examination reveals neuronal necrosis, perivascular lymphocytic infiltrates, and intranuclear eosinophilic inclusions within neurons and glial cells. These inclusions are described as being surrounded by a clear halo.

What is the most likely type of intranuclear inclusion body seen in this patient's brain biopsy?

(A) Negri bodies
(B) Lewy bodies
(C) Mallory bodies
(D) Cowdry type A bodies
(E) Russell bodies

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (HTN, hyperlipidemia).
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Acute onset (3 days) of headache, fever, confusion, focal seizures (right arm), lethargy, disorientation, right hemiparesis. This constellation points towards acute encephalitis with focal neurological deficits.
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast head CT shows hypodensity in the *left medial temporal lobe*. This localization is highly suggestive of HSV encephalitis.
- **CSF Analysis:** Cloudy CSF, elevated protein, decreased glucose, lymphocytic pleocytosis. These findings are consistent with viral meningitis/encephalitis.
- **CSF PCR:** Positive for HSV type 1. This confirms the diagnosis of HSV encephalitis.
- **Brain Biopsy:** Neuronal necrosis, perivascular lymphocytic infiltrates, and *intranuclear eosinophilic inclusions surrounded by a clear halo*. This specific description is the key histological finding.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific type of intranuclear inclusion body described.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **HSV Encephalitis:** Caused primarily by HSV-1 in immunocompetent adults. The virus typically spreads via olfactory or trigeminal nerves to the temporal lobes, causing hemorrhagic necrosis.
- **Pathogenesis:** Viral replication within neurons and glial cells leads to cell death and inflammation.
- **Histopathology:** Characteristic findings include neuronal necrosis, perivascular lymphocytic cuffing, microglial nodules, and *intranuclear inclusions*.
- **Intranuclear Inclusions:** These are eosinophilic (pink-staining) bodies found within the nucleus of infected cells. In HSV encephalitis, they are specifically described as *Cowdry type A bodies*, which are large, glassy, eosinophilic inclusions surrounded by a clear halo. They represent aggregates of viral proteins and nucleic acids.
- **Differential Diagnosis of Intranuclear Inclusions:** Different viral infections and pathological conditions are associated with distinct types of intranuclear inclusions.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Negri bodies:** These are eosinophilic, sharply outlined, pathognomonic inclusions found in the cytoplasm of neurons infected with the rabies virus. They are typically located near the nucleus but are cytoplasmic, not intranuclear, and are not surrounded by a halo. This patient's presentation (acute encephalitis, temporal lobe involvement, CSF findings, positive HSV PCR) is not consistent with rabies.
- (B) **Lewy bodies:** These are eosinophilic, spherical inclusions found primarily in the cytoplasm of neurons, particularly in the substantia nigra and cortex, characteristic of Parkinson's disease and Lewy body dementia. They are composed mainly of aggregated alpha-synuclein protein. They are cytoplasmic, not intranuclear, and are not associated with acute viral encephalitis.
- (C) **Mallory bodies:** These are eosinophilic, amorphous, ropey inclusions found in the cytoplasm of hepatocytes, typically associated with alcoholic liver disease or other forms of hepatocellular injury. They are composed of aggregated intermediate filaments (cytokeratins). They are cytoplasmic, not intranuclear, and found in the liver, not the brain.
- (E) **Russell bodies:** These are eosinophilic, spherical inclusions found in the cytoplasm of plasma cells, representing accumulations of immunoglobulins. They are cytoplasmic, not intranuclear, and are associated with chronic inflammation or plasma cell disorders, not acute viral encephalitis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Cowdry Type A Bodies (HSV) | Negri Bodies (Rabies) | Lewy Bodies (Parkinson's) | Mallory Bodies (Liver) | Russell Bodies (Plasma Cells) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------|------------------------|---------------------------|-------------------------|--------------------------------|
| **Location**         | Nucleus                     | Cytoplasm              | Cytoplasm                 | Cytoplasm               | Cytoplasm                      |
| **Cell Type**        | Neurons, Glial Cells        | Neurons                | Neurons                   | Hepatocytes             | Plasma Cells                   |
| **Appearance**       | Eosinophilic, glassy, halo  | Eosinophilic, round    | Eosinophilic, round       | Eosinophilic, ropey     | Eosinophilic, spherical        |
| **Associated Disease**| HSV Encephalitis            | Rabies                 | Parkinson's Disease       | Alcoholic Liver Disease | Plasma Cell Disorders           |
| **Halo**             | Present                     | Absent                 | Absent                    | Absent                  | Absent                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is recognizing the characteristic histopathological finding of Cowdry type A bodies (intranuclear eosinophilic inclusions with a clear halo) in the context of HSV encephalitis, which typically affects the temporal lobes. Differentiating these from other types of inclusions (Negri bodies, Lewy bodies, Mallory bodies, Russell bodies) based on their location (nuclear vs. cytoplasmic), morphology, and associated diseases is crucial.

<a id='question-056'></a>

### Question 056: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 02-06 (neurons and glial cells.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Demyelination in the central nervous system (CNS) caused by immune-mediated attack on myelin, leading to characteristic clinical and pathological findings. (中枢神经系统脱髓鞘，由免疫介导的髓鞘攻击引起，导致特征性的临床和病理发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the emergency department with acute onset of blurred vision in her left eye, accompanied by pain with eye movement. She reports similar, albeit milder, symptoms in her right eye three months prior, which resolved spontaneously. Her past medical history is unremarkable, and she denies any recent infections or vaccinations. Neurological examination reveals decreased visual acuity in the left eye, a relative afferent pupillary defect (RAPD) on the left, and nystagmus. Brain MRI with and without contrast shows multiple, ovoid, T2-hyperintense lesions scattered throughout the white matter of the brain and spinal cord, some demonstrating gadolinium enhancement. One lesion is located periventricularly adjacent to the left lateral ventricle, and another is present in the brainstem. Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis reveals mild pleocytosis (50 cells/µL, predominantly lymphocytes) and elevated IgG index. Visual evoked potentials (VEPs) show delayed P100 latency.

Which of the following pathological processes is most likely responsible for the observed clinical and imaging findings?

(A) Accumulation of misfolded proteins leading to neuronal dysfunction and cell death.
(B) Immune-mediated destruction of oligodendrocytes and myelin sheaths.
(C) Formation of amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles.
(D) Demyelination secondary to direct viral infection of glial cells.
(E) Ischemic necrosis of neurons and glial cells due to vascular occlusion.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old female. This age and sex are typical for the onset of multiple sclerosis (MS).
- **Symptoms:** Acute onset blurred vision in one eye (optic neuritis) with pain on eye movement, history of similar transient symptoms in the other eye. Optic neuritis is a classic presenting symptom of MS. The relapsing-remitting nature (previous episode resolved) is also characteristic.
- **Neurological Exam:** Decreased visual acuity, RAPD (indicating optic nerve dysfunction), nystagmus (suggesting brainstem or cerebellar involvement). These findings correlate with the patient's symptoms and point towards CNS pathology.
- **MRI Findings:** Multiple, ovoid, T2-hyperintense lesions in the white matter of the brain and spinal cord, some enhancing with gadolinium. This pattern (dissemination in space and time, periventricular location, Dawson's fingers) is highly suggestive of MS. Enhancement indicates active inflammation.
- **CSF Analysis:** Mild lymphocytic pleocytosis and elevated IgG index. This indicates intrathecal immunoglobulin synthesis, consistent with an inflammatory/immune process within the CNS, typical for MS.
- **VEPs:** Delayed P100 latency. This confirms demyelination affecting the optic nerve pathway, even if subclinical.
- **Overall Picture:** The constellation of symptoms (optic neuritis, relapsing-remitting course), neurological signs, characteristic MRI findings (dissemination in space and time, periventricular lesions), and CSF findings (oligoclonal bands/elevated IgG index) strongly points towards a diagnosis of Multiple Sclerosis (MS).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying pathological process. MS is fundamentally an autoimmune disease targeting myelin in the CNS.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Multiple Sclerosis (MS):** An autoimmune inflammatory disease characterized by demyelination in the CNS.
- **Pathogenesis:** Autoimmune T cells (primarily CD4+ Th1 and Th17 cells) cross the blood-brain barrier and attack myelin sheaths produced by oligodendrocytes. B cells and antibodies also play a role.
- **Mechanism:** The immune attack leads to destruction of myelin (demyelination) and damage to oligodendrocytes (oligodendrocyte injury/death) and axons. This disrupts nerve impulse conduction, leading to neurological deficits.
- **Histopathology:** Lesions (plaques) show areas of demyelination (loss of myelin staining, e.g., with Luxol fast blue), inflammation (lymphocyte infiltration, macrophages), gliosis (astrocytosis), and axonal damage.
- **Molecular Targets:** Myelin proteins (e.g., myelin basic protein, proteolipid protein) are recognized as autoantigens. The exact trigger for autoimmunity is unknown but likely involves genetic predisposition and environmental factors.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Demyelination disrupts saltatory conduction, leading to slowed nerve conduction velocity (e.g., delayed VEPs) and neurological symptoms depending on the location of the lesions (e.g., optic nerve -> optic neuritis; brainstem -> nystagmus, diplopia; spinal cord -> weakness, sensory changes).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Accumulation of misfolded proteins leading to neuronal dysfunction and cell death. This describes diseases like Alzheimer's disease (amyloid-beta, tau), Parkinson's disease (alpha-synuclein), Huntington's disease (huntingtin), or prion diseases (PrPSc). These typically present with progressive neurodegeneration, cognitive decline, or movement disorders, not the relapsing-remitting, multifocal demyelinating pattern seen here.
- (C) Formation of amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles. This is the hallmark pathology of Alzheimer's disease, characterized by progressive cognitive decline and memory loss, not acute optic neuritis or multifocal white matter lesions.
- (D) Demyelination secondary to direct viral infection of glial cells. While some viral infections (e.g., PML caused by JC virus, progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy) can cause demyelination, they typically occur in immunocompromised individuals and have distinct pathological features (e.g., enlarged oligodendrocytes with intranuclear inclusions in PML). The patient's history and CSF findings (lymphocytic pleocytosis, elevated IgG index) are more consistent with an autoimmune process than a direct viral infection. Acute disseminated encephalomyelitis (ADEM) is a post-infectious autoimmune demyelinating disease, but it's usually monophasic and often follows a clear infection, which is not mentioned here.
- (E) Ischemic necrosis of neurons and glial cells due to vascular occlusion. This describes stroke or infarction. While strokes can cause neurological deficits, the MRI findings (multiple, ovoid, T2-hyperintense lesions, often periventricular) and the relapsing-remitting clinical course are not typical for stroke. Stroke lesions are typically wedge-shaped, involve specific vascular territories, and show cytotoxic edema acutely.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Multiple Sclerosis (MS)                                  | Alzheimer's Disease (AD)                               | Viral Demyelination (e.g., PML)                          | Stroke (Ischemia)                                     |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Pathology**   | Immune-mediated demyelination & oligodendrocyte damage | Misfolded protein aggregates (amyloid, tau)             | Direct viral infection & demyelination                  | Vascular occlusion & infarction                         |
| **Typical Age**         | 20-40 years                                             | >65 years                                              | Variable, often immunocompromised                      | Variable, often >50 years                               |
| **Clinical Course**     | Relapsing-remitting or progressive                      | Progressive cognitive decline                            | Acute/subacute progressive neurological deficits        | Acute onset focal neurological deficits                 |
| **Common Symptoms**     | Optic neuritis, sensory changes, weakness, ataxia        | Memory loss, cognitive impairment, behavioral changes | Hemiparesis, visual field defects, cognitive changes | Hemiparesis, aphasia, sensory loss, visual field defects |
| **MRI Findings**        | Multiple, ovoid, periventricular T2 lesions, enhancement | Cortical atrophy, hippocampal atrophy                  | Focal, asymmetric white matter lesions, no enhancement | Wedge-shaped lesions in vascular territories, DWI changes |
| **CSF Findings**        | Oligoclonal bands, elevated IgG index                   | Usually normal                                          | Variable, may show viral markers                       | Usually normal                                          |
| **Key Pathology**       | Perivascular lymphocytic infiltrates, demyelination      | Amyloid plaques, neurofibrillary tangles                | Enlarged oligodendrocytes, intranuclear inclusions     | Neuronal necrosis, gliosis                              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Multiple sclerosis is an autoimmune disease characterized by immune-mediated demyelination in the CNS, leading to multifocal neurological deficits that often follow a relapsing-remitting course. Key diagnostic features include optic neuritis, characteristic MRI lesions (dissemination in space and time), and CSF findings suggestive of intrathecal inflammation. Differentiate MS from other causes of demyelination (e.g., PML, ADEM) and other neurological diseases (e.g., stroke, neurodegenerative diseases) based on clinical presentation, imaging, and CSF findings.

<a id='question-057'></a>

### Question 057: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 02-07 (• Rabies has characteristic Negri bodies in the cy)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Rabies virus infection leads to characteristic neuronal cytoplasmic inclusions called Negri bodies, which are eosinophilic and contain viral nucleocapsids. (狂犬病毒感染导致特征性的神经元胞浆内包涵体，称为内格里小体，呈嗜酸性，含有病毒核衣壳。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male farmer presents to the emergency department after being bitten by a stray dog two weeks prior. He reports that he did not seek medical attention immediately after the bite, as it seemed minor. Over the past few days, he has developed progressively worsening hydrophobia (fear of water), aerophobia (fear of drafts or fresh air), agitation, confusion, and muscle spasms, particularly in his pharynx and larynx, making swallowing difficult. His vital signs show tachycardia (110 bpm) and tachypnea (24 breaths/min). Neurological examination reveals hyperreflexia, generalized muscle rigidity, and intermittent episodes of opisthotonos. A lumbar puncture shows elevated protein levels (150 mg/dL, normal <45 mg/dL) and lymphocytic pleocytosis (50 cells/µL, normal <5 cells/µL), with normal glucose levels. A brain biopsy is performed. Histopathological examination of the brain tissue reveals neuronal cell death and the presence of numerous eosinophilic, sharply outlined, intracytoplasmic inclusions within pyramidal neurons of the hippocampus and Purkinje cells of the cerebellum. These inclusions are round to oval, measure approximately 15-20 µm in diameter, and have a central clear halo.

What is the most likely specific histological finding responsible for the patient's neurological symptoms?

(A) Lewy bodies, composed of aggregated alpha-synuclein, primarily found in the substantia nigra and cortex.
(B) Pick bodies, composed of aggregated tau protein, typically found in Pick disease affecting the frontal and temporal lobes.
(C) Negri bodies, composed of viral nucleocapsids, characteristically found in the cytoplasm of neurons, particularly in the hippocampus and cerebellum.
(D) Mallory bodies, composed of aggregated cytokeratin filaments, typically found in hepatocytes undergoing degeneration in alcoholic liver disease.
(E) Russell bodies, composed of aggregated immunoglobulins, found in the cytoplasm of plasma cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male farmer, bitten by a stray dog 2 weeks ago. This establishes a potential exposure risk for zoonotic diseases, specifically rabies transmitted via animal bite.
*   **Symptoms:** Progressive hydrophobia, aerophobia, agitation, confusion, muscle spasms (pharyngeal/laryngeal), hyperreflexia, rigidity, opisthotonos. These are classic symptoms of acute encephalitic rabies, indicating severe CNS involvement. The incubation period (2 weeks) is consistent with rabies.
*   **CSF Findings:** Elevated protein, lymphocytic pleocytosis, normal glucose. This suggests CNS inflammation, consistent with viral encephalitis.
*   **Brain Biopsy Findings:** Neuronal cell death, eosinophilic intracytoplasmic inclusions (15-20 µm, central clear halo) in hippocampal pyramidal neurons and cerebellar Purkinje cells. This description is pathognomonic for Negri bodies.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific histological finding responsible for the symptoms, directly linking the clinical presentation and biopsy findings to a specific pathological entity.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Rabies Virus:** An RNA virus (Lyssavirus genus) that infects the CNS after entering through peripheral nerves (often from a bite).
*   **Pathogenesis:** The virus replicates in muscle cells at the bite site and then travels retrogradely along axons to the CNS. It infects neurons, causing neuronal dysfunction and death.
*   **Negri Bodies:** These are characteristic eosinophilic inclusions found in the cytoplasm of infected neurons, particularly in the hippocampus (CA1 pyramidal neurons), cerebellum (Purkinje cells), brainstem, and spinal cord. They represent aggregates of viral nucleocapsids (viral RNA complexed with the nucleoprotein). Their presence is diagnostic of rabies infection post-mortem, although they may not always be present or easily found in all cases. The clinical symptoms arise from the viral damage to specific brain regions controlling autonomic function, motor control, and behavior.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Lewy bodies:** Associated with Parkinson's disease and Dementia with Lewy bodies. Composed of aggregated alpha-synuclein. Found primarily in the substantia nigra (leading to motor symptoms) and cortex (leading to cognitive symptoms). The clinical presentation (hydrophobia, aerophobia, spasms) and location (hippocampus, cerebellum) do not match Lewy body disease.
*   **(B) Pick bodies:** Associated with Pick disease, a subtype of frontotemporal dementia. Composed of aggregated tau protein (specifically 3R tau isoforms). Found in neurons of the frontal and temporal lobes, leading to behavioral and language changes. The clinical presentation and location (hippocampus, cerebellum) do not match Pick disease.
*   **(D) Mallory bodies:** Found in hepatocytes, typically in alcoholic liver disease or other forms of liver injury. Composed of aggregated cytokeratin filaments. They are a sign of hepatocellular injury/degeneration, not a CNS finding related to the patient's symptoms.
*   **(E) Russell bodies:** Found in plasma cells, particularly in chronic inflammation or plasma cell dyscrasias (like multiple myeloma). Composed of aggregated immunoglobulins. They are cytoplasmic inclusions within plasma cells, not neuronal inclusions causing CNS symptoms like hydrophobia.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Negri Bodies (Rabies)                                  | Lewy Bodies (Parkinson's/DLB)                       | Pick Bodies (Pick Disease)                          | Mallory Bodies (Liver Injury)                     | Russell Bodies (Plasma Cells)                    |
| :------------------ | :----------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------ |
| **Location**        | CNS neurons (hippocampus, cerebellum, brainstem)       | CNS neurons (Substantia Nigra, Cortex)             | CNS neurons (Frontal/Temporal Lobes)               | Hepatocytes                                       | Plasma Cells                                     |
| **Composition**     | Viral nucleocapsids (RNA + protein)                    | Aggregated alpha-synuclein                          | Aggregated tau protein (3R isoforms)                | Aggregated cytokeratin                             | Aggregated immunoglobulins                       |
| **Appearance**      | Eosinophilic, intracytoplasmic, central clear halo     | Eosinophilic, intracytoplasmic, often ringed       | Eosinophilic, intracytoplasmic, often silver-stain+ | Eosinophilic, intracytoplasmic, ropey appearance | Eosinophilic, intracytoplasmic, perinuclear location |
| **Associated Disease** | Rabies                                                | Parkinson's disease, Dementia with Lewy bodies      | Pick disease (Frontotemporal Dementia)             | Alcoholic hepatitis, other liver injury           | Chronic inflammation, Plasma cell dyscrasias      |
| **Clinical Symptoms** | Hydrophobia, aerophobia, agitation, spasms, paralysis | Bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, cognitive decline | Behavioral changes, language deficits              | Liver failure symptoms                             | Variable, related to underlying condition        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Rabies virus infection causes characteristic eosinophilic intracytoplasmic inclusions called Negri bodies in neurons, particularly in the hippocampus and cerebellum. These findings, along with the classic clinical presentation of hydrophobia and aerophobia following an animal bite, are diagnostic of rabies. Differentiate Negri bodies from other neuronal inclusions like Lewy bodies (alpha-synuclein, Parkinson's) and Pick bodies (tau, FTD), and non-neuronal inclusions like Mallory bodies (cytokeratin, liver) and Russell bodies (immunoglobulins, plasma cells).

<a id='question-058'></a>

### Question 058: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 02-08 (and Purkinje cells.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology, genetic basis, and histological features of Friedreich's ataxia, specifically focusing on the role of frataxin deficiency and its impact on mitochondrial function and neuronal degeneration, particularly affecting the Purkinje cells in the cerebellum. (本题考察弗里德里希共济失调症的病理生理学、遗传学基础和组织学特征，特别是关注弗拉他كس蛋白缺乏对线粒体功能和神经元变性的影响，尤其影响小脑中的浦肯野细胞。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 12-year-old boy presents to his pediatrician with a 3-year history of progressive gait ataxia, slurred speech (dysarthria), and difficulty coordinating movements (dysmetria). His parents report that he frequently trips and falls. He also has developed hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and diabetes mellitus. Neurological examination reveals bilateral lower extremity areflexia, positive Babinski signs, and impaired finger-to-nose testing. MRI of the brain shows cerebellar atrophy, particularly affecting the vermis, and T2-weighted hyperintensity in the corticospinal tracts. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous GAA repeat expansion in the *FXN* gene. A muscle biopsy shows ragged red fibers on Gomori trichrome stain. Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most directly responsible for the neuronal degeneration observed in this patient?

(A) Impaired degradation of mutant huntingtin protein aggregates.
(B) Reduced synthesis of frataxin protein leading to mitochondrial dysfunction.
(C) Defective glycosylation of alpha-dystroglycan leading to muscle and brain abnormalities.
(D) Accumulation of alpha-synuclein aggregates causing Lewy body formation.
(E) Defective transport of apolipoprotein E leading to lipoprotein abnormalities.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 12-year-old boy. Age of onset is typical for Friedreich's ataxia (FA).
*   **Symptoms:** Progressive gait ataxia, dysarthria, dysmetria (cerebellar signs), hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, diabetes mellitus (systemic features). These are classic features of FA.
*   **Neurological Exam:** Lower extremity areflexia, positive Babinski signs (upper motor neuron signs), impaired finger-to-nose (cerebellar signs). This combination points towards spinocerebellar degeneration.
*   **Imaging:** Cerebellar atrophy (especially vermis) and T2 hyperintensity in corticospinal tracts. These findings are characteristic of FA.
*   **Genetics:** Homozygous GAA repeat expansion in the *FXN* gene. This confirms the diagnosis of FA. The *FXN* gene encodes frataxin.
*   **Muscle Biopsy:** Ragged red fibers (Gomori trichrome). This indicates mitochondrial dysfunction, a known consequence of frataxin deficiency.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the *molecular mechanism* responsible for neuronal degeneration.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Friedreich's Ataxia (FA):** An autosomal recessive neurodegenerative disorder.
*   **Genetic Basis:** Caused by a homozygous expansion of a GAA trinucleotide repeat in the first intron of the *FXN* gene on chromosome 9. This expansion leads to reduced transcription of the *FXN* gene.
*   **Frataxin Protein:** The *FXN* gene encodes frataxin, a mitochondrial protein.
*   **Function of Frataxin:** Frataxin is crucial for iron-sulfur cluster (ISC) biogenesis and mitochondrial iron homeostasis. It plays a role in mitochondrial iron-sulfur cluster assembly, oxidative stress defense, and mitochondrial dynamics.
*   **Pathophysiology:** Reduced frataxin levels lead to impaired ISC synthesis, accumulation of iron in mitochondria, increased oxidative stress, impaired mitochondrial respiration (especially complex I), and ultimately mitochondrial dysfunction. This dysfunction particularly affects neurons, especially Purkinje cells in the cerebellum and dorsal root ganglia neurons, leading to their degeneration. The cardiomyopathy and diabetes are also linked to mitochondrial dysfunction in the heart and pancreas, respectively.
*   **Neuronal Degeneration:** The degeneration of Purkinje cells in the cerebellum explains the ataxia and dysmetria. Degeneration of neurons in the spinocerebellar tracts and dorsal root ganglia explains the areflexia and sensory neuropathy.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Impaired degradation of mutant huntingtin protein aggregates:** This describes the mechanism of Huntington's disease. Huntington's disease is caused by a CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene, leading to a mutant huntingtin protein that forms aggregates and causes neuronal death, primarily in the basal ganglia (striatum). This is incorrect for FA.
*   **(C) Defective glycosylation of alpha-dystroglycan leading to muscle and brain abnormalities:** This describes the mechanism of congenital muscular dystrophies (e.g., Walker-Warburg syndrome, Fukuyama congenital muscular dystrophy). These are caused by mutations affecting the glycosylation of alpha-dystroglycan, a protein essential for linking the extracellular matrix to the cytoskeleton. This leads to muscle weakness, brain malformations, and eye abnormalities. This is incorrect for FA.
*   **(D) Accumulation of alpha-synuclein aggregates causing Lewy body formation:** This describes the mechanism of Parkinson's disease and Lewy body dementia. These are characterized by the accumulation of misfolded alpha-synuclein protein into Lewy bodies, primarily affecting dopaminergic neurons in the substantia nigra (Parkinson's) or more widespread cortical and brainstem neurons (Lewy body dementia). This is incorrect for FA.
*   **(E) Defective transport of apolipoprotein E leading to lipoprotein abnormalities:** This describes the mechanism of Abetalipoproteinemia (Bassen-Kornzweig syndrome). This is caused by mutations in the *MTTP* gene, which encodes a protein required for the assembly and secretion of chylomicrons and VLDL. This leads to fat malabsorption, vitamin deficiencies (especially vitamin E), and neurological problems (ataxia, peripheral neuropathy, retinitis pigmentosa) due to vitamin E deficiency affecting neuronal membranes. While ataxia is present, the underlying genetic and molecular cause is completely different from FA. This is incorrect for FA.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Friedreich's Ataxia (FA)                                  | Huntington's Disease (HD)                               | Congenital Muscular Dystrophy (CMD)                     | Parkinson's Disease (PD) / Lewy Body Dementia (LBD) | Abetalipoproteinemia (Bassen-Kornzweig)                 |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Genetic Cause**       | GAA repeat expansion in *FXN* gene                         | CAG repeat expansion in *HTT* gene                       | Defects in alpha-dystroglycan glycosylation             | Alpha-synuclein gene duplication/mutation (*SNCA*)     | *MTTP* gene mutation                                    |
| **Protein Affected**    | Frataxin                                                  | Huntingtin                                              | Alpha-dystroglycan                                       | Alpha-synuclein                                        | Apolipoprotein B (via MTTP)                            |
| **Primary Pathology**   | Mitochondrial dysfunction, ISC deficiency, oxidative stress | Mutant protein aggregates, neuronal death (striatum)     | Muscle & brain malformations, defective ECM linkage     | Lewy bodies (alpha-synuclein aggregates)               | Fat malabsorption, Vitamin E deficiency, neuropathy    |
| **Key Clinical Features** | Ataxia, cardiomyopathy, diabetes, areflexia, scoliosis    | Chorea, cognitive decline, psychiatric symptoms          | Muscle weakness, brain malformations, eye abnormalities | Bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, cognitive decline     | Ataxia, peripheral neuropathy, retinitis pigmentosa     |
| **Affected Cells**      | Purkinje cells, DRG neurons, cardiomyocytes, pancreatic β-cells | Medium spiny neurons (striatum)                         | Muscle fibers, brain neurons                            | Dopaminergic neurons (SN), cortical/brainstem neurons | Neurons (due to Vit E deficiency), muscle, enterocytes |
| **Molecular Mechanism** | Reduced frataxin -> mitochondrial dysfunction              | Mutant huntingtin aggregates -> neuronal death           | Defective glycosylation -> ECM/cytoskeleton disruption | Alpha-synuclein aggregates -> proteasomal dysfunction | Defective lipoprotein transport -> Vit E deficiency    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Friedreich's ataxia is caused by reduced frataxin protein due to a GAA repeat expansion, leading to mitochondrial dysfunction and neuronal degeneration, particularly affecting the cerebellum and spinal cord. Key differentials include other spinocerebellar ataxias (like Huntington's, which involves huntingtin aggregates) and disorders with ataxia but different underlying mechanisms (like abetalipoproteinemia, which involves vitamin E deficiency). Remember the classic triad of ataxia, cardiomyopathy, and diabetes in FA.

<a id='question-059'></a>

### Question 059: Central Nervous System Pathology: HIV Encephalopathy
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: HIV-associated neurocognitive disorder (HAND) / HIV encephalopathy is characterized by microglial nodules (MGN) and multinucleated giant cells (MNGC) in the brain parenchyma, resulting from chronic inflammation and macrophage infection with HIV-1. (HIV 相关神经认知障碍/HIV 脑病以脑实质内的微胶质细胞结节 (MGN) 和多核巨细胞 (MNGC) 为特征，这是由慢性炎症和巨噬细胞感染 HIV-1 引起的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male with a 15-year history of HIV infection, currently on antiretroviral therapy (ART) with undetectable viral load for the past 5 years, presents with progressive cognitive decline, gait disturbance, and urinary incontinence over the past 6 months. His CD4 count is 350 cells/µL, and his HIV RNA level is <50 copies/mL. Neurological examination reveals spasticity, hyperreflexia, and impaired executive function. MRI of the brain shows diffuse white matter hyperintensities and atrophy, particularly in the frontal lobes. A brain biopsy is performed. Histopathological examination reveals perivascular lymphocytic infiltrates, numerous microglial nodules (MGN) containing CD68+ macrophages, and multinucleated giant cells (MNGC) positive for both CD68 and HIV-1 p24 antigen.

Which of the following pathological findings is MOST characteristic of the underlying mechanism causing this patient's neurological symptoms?

(A) Formation of syncytia via fusion of infected cells expressing gp120 with uninfected cells expressing CD4 and CCR5.
(B) Deposition of amyloid-beta plaques and neurofibrillary tangles in the cerebral cortex and hippocampus.
(C) Formation of microglial nodules (MGN) and multinucleated giant cells (MNGC) resulting from chronic macrophage infection and inflammation.
(D) Demyelination of axons in the central nervous system mediated by cytotoxic T lymphocytes targeting myelin basic protein.
(E) Formation of Lewy bodies composed of aggregated alpha-synuclein within neuronal cell bodies.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male, 15-year HIV history, on ART (undetectable viral load for 5 years), CD4 350 cells/µL. This suggests a history of significant immune compromise despite current ART effectiveness.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Progressive cognitive decline, gait disturbance, urinary incontinence (suggestive of frontal lobe dysfunction and corticospinal tract involvement).
- **Neurological Exam:** Spasticity, hyperreflexia (upper motor neuron signs), impaired executive function (frontal lobe dysfunction).
- **MRI Findings:** Diffuse white matter hyperintensities, frontal lobe atrophy (consistent with HIV-associated neurocognitive disorder - HAND).
- **Brain Biopsy Findings:** Perivascular lymphocytic infiltrates, numerous microglial nodules (MGN) containing CD68+ macrophages, and multinucleated giant cells (MNGC) positive for CD68 and HIV-1 p24 antigen. This is the key pathological finding.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST characteristic pathological finding underlying the mechanism.

The vignette describes a patient with HAND, likely HIV-associated dementia (HAD), despite being on ART with a suppressed viral load. The brain biopsy findings are classic for HAND: MGN and MNGC containing HIV-infected macrophages. The question asks for the pathological finding characteristic of the *mechanism* causing these symptoms. Option (C) directly describes the key histological features (MGN and MNGC) resulting from the underlying mechanism (chronic macrophage infection and inflammation).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **HIV Encephalopathy Pathogenesis:** HIV primarily infects microglia and macrophages in the CNS. These cells express CD4 and chemokine receptors (CCR5 is the main co-receptor for macrophage-tropic HIV-1 strains). Infected macrophages become activated, releasing pro-inflammatory cytokines (TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6), chemokines, and reactive oxygen species. This chronic inflammation contributes to neuronal dysfunction and damage.
- **Microglial Nodules (MGN):** Clusters of activated microglia and macrophages, often containing HIV-infected cells. They are a hallmark of HAND.
- **Multinucleated Giant Cells (MNGC):** Formed by the fusion of multiple infected macrophages, often containing numerous HIV-infected nuclei. They are also characteristic of HAND.
- **Mechanism:** The formation of MGN and MNGC is a direct consequence of HIV infection of CNS macrophages (microglia and perivascular macrophages), leading to chronic inflammation and cellular dysfunction. This process is central to the pathogenesis of HAND.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Formation of syncytia via fusion of infected cells expressing gp120 with uninfected cells expressing CD4 and CCR5. This describes a mechanism of HIV cell-to-cell transmission and infection spread, particularly relevant in lymphoid tissues and potentially contributing to CNS infection, but it is not the *most characteristic pathological finding* described in the biopsy (MGN/MNGC). Syncytia formation is less prominent in the CNS pathology compared to MGN/MNGC.
- (B): Deposition of amyloid-beta plaques and neurofibrillary tangles in the cerebral cortex and hippocampus. This describes the hallmark pathology of Alzheimer's disease, not HIV encephalopathy. While HAND can sometimes coexist with Alzheimer's pathology, it is not the primary or characteristic finding in this case.
- (D): Demyelination of axons in the central nervous system mediated by cytotoxic T lymphocytes targeting myelin basic protein. This describes the pathology of multiple sclerosis or other demyelinating diseases. While some white matter changes occur in HAND (seen on MRI), the primary pathology is not immune-mediated demyelination targeting myelin basic protein.
- (E): Formation of Lewy bodies composed of aggregated alpha-synuclein within neuronal cell bodies. This describes the hallmark pathology of Parkinson's disease and dementia with Lewy bodies. It is unrelated to HIV encephalopathy.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | HIV Encephalopathy (HAND)                                  | Alzheimer's Disease                     | Multiple Sclerosis                     | Parkinson's Disease                     |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Pathology**   | Microglial nodules (MGN), Multinucleated Giant Cells (MNGC) | Amyloid plaques, Neurofibrillary tangles | Demyelination, Perivascular inflammation | Lewy bodies (α-synuclein)               |
| **Key Cells Involved**  | Microglia, Macrophages                                      | Neurons, Astrocytes                     | Oligodendrocytes, Lymphocytes           | Neurons (dopaminergic)                  |
| **Characteristic Lesion** | MGN/MNGC containing HIV p24                               | Plaques/Tangles                         | Plaques (demyelination)                 | Lewy bodies                             |
| **Clinical Features**   | Cognitive decline, motor deficits, behavioral changes        | Memory loss, cognitive decline          | Motor/Sensory deficits, optic neuritis   | Tremor, rigidity, bradykinesia           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
HIV encephalopathy (HAND) is characterized histologically by microglial nodules (MGN) and multinucleated giant cells (MNGC) in the brain parenchyma, resulting from chronic infection and inflammation of CNS macrophages. Differentiate this from Alzheimer's disease (plaques/tangles), Multiple Sclerosis (demyelination), and Parkinson's disease (Lewy bodies). The key pathological features in HAND are the MGN and MNGC.
