<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 02-50 (Medical Genetics)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the molecular basis and clinical presentation of lysosomal storage diseases, specifically Tay-Sachs disease, focusing on the deficient enzyme, accumulated substrate, and resulting neuropathology. (考察溶酶体贮积症的分子基础和临床表现，特别是泰-萨克斯病，重点是缺乏的酶、积累的底物和由此产生的神经病理学。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old infant presents to the pediatrician with developmental regression, hypotonia, and an exaggerated startle response to loud noises. The infant was previously meeting developmental milestones appropriately but has shown a marked decline in motor skills over the past two months. Family history is significant for a deceased sibling who died in early childhood from an unspecified neurological disorder. On physical examination, the infant exhibits generalized muscle weakness, nystagmus, and a cherry-red spot on funduscopic examination. Laboratory testing reveals markedly elevated levels of GM2 ganglioside in the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF). Genetic testing confirms homozygosity for a pathogenic mutation in the *HEXA* gene.

Which of the following molecular targets is deficient in this patient, leading to the accumulation of GM2 ganglioside?

(A) Sphingomyelinase
(B) Hexosaminidase A
(C) Glucocerebrosidase
(D) Galactocerebrosidase
(E) Alpha-galactosidase A

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-month-old infant. This age is typical for the onset of infantile Tay-Sachs disease.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Developmental regression, hypotonia, exaggerated startle response. These are classic signs of progressive neurodegeneration in infancy.
- **Family History:** Deceased sibling with neurological disorder. Suggests a possible inherited condition, likely autosomal recessive given the presentation.
- **Physical Exam:** Nystagmus, cherry-red spot on fundoscopy. The cherry-red spot is a highly characteristic finding in several lysosomal storage diseases, including Tay-Sachs, due to the accumulation of storage material in the retinal ganglion cells surrounding the fovea.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated GM2 ganglioside in CSF. This points directly to a defect in the degradation pathway of GM2 ganglioside.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygosity for *HEXA* gene mutation. The *HEXA* gene encodes the alpha subunit of the enzyme hexosaminidase A.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the deficient molecular target leading to GM2 ganglioside accumulation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- Tay-Sachs disease is an autosomal recessive lysosomal storage disorder.
- It is caused by mutations in the *HEXA* gene, which encodes the alpha subunit of the enzyme hexosaminidase A. The beta subunit is encoded by the *HEXB* gene.
- Hexosaminidase A is a lysosomal enzyme responsible for the hydrolysis of GM2 ganglioside into GM3 ganglioside and N-acetylgalactosamine.
- Deficiency of hexosaminidase A leads to the progressive accumulation of GM2 ganglioside, primarily within the lysosomes of neurons, particularly in the central nervous system.
- The accumulation of GM2 ganglioside disrupts neuronal function and leads to progressive neurodegeneration, manifesting clinically as developmental regression, hypotonia, seizures, blindness, and eventually death in early childhood.
- The cherry-red spot is caused by the contrast between the pale, lipid-laden retinal ganglion cells and the normal red color of the underlying choroid, visible through the fovea which lacks ganglion cells.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Sphingomyelinase: Deficiency of sphingomyelinase causes Niemann-Pick disease (types A and B). This leads to the accumulation of sphingomyelin, not GM2 ganglioside. Clinical features include hepatosplenomegaly, developmental delay, and sometimes a cherry-red spot, but the primary accumulated lipid is different.
- (C) Glucocerebrosidase: Deficiency of glucocerebrosidase causes Gaucher disease. This leads to the accumulation of glucocerebroside, primarily in macrophages (Gaucher cells) found in the spleen, liver, bone marrow, and brain (in neuronopathic forms). Clinical features vary widely but often include hepatosplenomegaly, bone disease, and cytopenias. It does not cause GM2 ganglioside accumulation.
- (D) Galactocerebrosidase: Deficiency of galactocerebrosidase causes Krabbe disease (globoid cell leukodystrophy). This leads to the accumulation of galactocerebroside and psychosine, primarily affecting oligodendrocytes and leading to demyelination. Clinical features include progressive neurodegeneration, irritability, hypertonia, and optic atrophy, typically presenting in infancy. It does not cause GM2 ganglioside accumulation.
- (E) Alpha-galactosidase A: Deficiency of alpha-galactosidase A causes Fabry disease. This is an X-linked lysosomal storage disorder leading to the accumulation of globotriaosylceramide (Gb3) in various tissues, including endothelial cells, smooth muscle cells, and neurons. Clinical features include angiokeratomas, peripheral neuropathy, renal failure, and cardiovascular disease. It does not cause GM2 ganglioside accumulation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Disease          | Deficient Enzyme       | Accumulated Substrate | Key Clinical Features                                  | Genetic Inheritance |
|-------------------|------------------------|-----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|---------------------|
| Tay-Sachs         | Hexosaminidase A       | GM2 ganglioside       | Developmental regression, cherry-red spot, hypotonia | Autosomal Recessive |
| Niemann-Pick (A/B) | Sphingomyelinase       | Sphingomyelin         | Hepatosplenomegaly, neurodegeneration, cherry-red spot | Autosomal Recessive |
| Gaucher           | Glucocerebrosidase     | Glucocerebroside      | Hepatosplenomegaly, bone disease, cytopenias          | Autosomal Recessive |
| Krabbe            | Galactocerebrosidase   | Galactocerebroside    | Leukodystrophy, hypertonia, optic atrophy             | Autosomal Recessive |
| Fabry             | Alpha-galactosidase A | Globotriaosylceramide | Angiokeratomas, neuropathy, renal failure             | X-linked            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Tay-Sachs disease is a classic example of a lysosomal storage disorder caused by a specific enzyme deficiency (Hexosaminidase A) leading to the accumulation of a specific substrate (GM2 ganglioside) and characteristic clinical features (neurodegeneration, cherry-red spot). Differentiating it from other lysosomal storage diseases requires knowledge of the specific enzyme and substrate involved in each disorder. Key differentials include Niemann-Pick disease (sphingomyelin accumulation), Gaucher disease (glucocerebroside accumulation), and Krabbe disease (galactocerebroside accumulation).
